Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.

Though sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.

It’s not like I’m doing anything terribly important, but I’m up and moving from one thing to the next all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and switching between tasks is causing you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit. At home my husband gets frustrated because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then I’ve gone off into three other things, and he ends up checking behind me.\n\nAnd at the community center too—oh, they’re lovely people, really, we had that bake table last spring and everyone was so patient—but lately I can tell the other volunteers don’t quite trust me to keep the plans straight unless someone else looks them over. That feels embarrassing. It’s not disastrous, but it does cause strain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. hard for me to keep quiet, because even at knitting I end up chatting, humming, or getting up to look for supplies instead of just sitting calmly.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the pharmacy or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting makes me fidgety, and I catch myself sighing or jumping in too soon.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: My husband says I cut him off before he finishes, and I do the same in group planning because I’m afraid I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.

Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.

It’s not like I’m doing anything terribly important, but I’m up and moving from one thing to the next all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and switching between tasks is causing you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.

At the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.

At home my husband gets frustrated because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then I’ve gone off into three other things, and he ends up checking behind me.\n\nAnd at the community center too—oh, they’re lovely people, really, we had that bake table last spring and everyone was so patient—but lately I can tell the other volunteers don’t quite trust me to keep the plans straight unless someone else looks them over. That feels embarrassing. It’s not disastrous, but it does cause strain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?

My sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.

I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings or difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?\n\nMy sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.

But it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.

So I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.

I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings or difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?\n\nMy sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a social activity because of your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.\n\nBut it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?

And at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. Plans, times, who’s bringing what. I don’t think they’re trying to be mean. It’s just that they can’t really count on me to keep it all straight without someone checking behind me.

So yes, it’s caused strain. Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just feel like I’m always going, even when there’s no real reason to be. Like on a quiet day, I’ll start with the garden club list, then somehow I’m into my knitting bag, and then I’m sorting travel papers before I’ve actually finished any of it. It’s not like I’m doing anything terribly important, but I’m up and moving from one thing to the next all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and switching between tasks is causing you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit. At home my husband gets frustrated because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then I’ve gone off into three other things, and he ends up checking behind me.\n\nAnd at the community center too—oh, they’re lovely people, really, we had that bake table last spring and everyone was so patient—but lately I can tell the other volunteers don’t quite trust me to keep the plans straight unless someone else looks them over. That feels embarrassing. It’s not disastrous, but it does cause strain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings or difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?\n\nMy sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a social activity because of your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.\n\nBut it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?\n\nAnd at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. Plans, times, who’s bringing what. I don’t think they’re trying to be mean. It’s just that they can’t really count on me to keep it all straight without someone checking behind me.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused strain. Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed these difficulties with focusing and restlessness continuing into your adult life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even back in grade school, my teachers told my mother I was bright but scattered, always losing papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before I finished my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose they have. Though when you say “adult life,” it makes me think back even farther, honestly. In grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered — that was the phrase, “bright but scattered.” I was always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work.

My mother kept some of those report cards in a drawer with old birthday cards and recipes clipped from the newspaper — she saved everything. But yes, the same sort of thing didn’t just disappear when I grew up.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just feel like I’m always going, even when there’s no real reason to be. Like on a quiet day, I’ll start with the garden club list, then somehow I’m into my knitting bag, and then I’m sorting travel papers before I’ve actually finished any of it. It’s not like I’m doing anything terribly important, but I’m up and moving from one thing to the next all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and switching between tasks is causing you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit. At home my husband gets frustrated because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then I’ve gone off into three other things, and he ends up checking behind me.\n\nAnd at the community center too—oh, they’re lovely people, really, we had that bake table last spring and everyone was so patient—but lately I can tell the other volunteers don’t quite trust me to keep the plans straight unless someone else looks them over. That feels embarrassing. It’s not disastrous, but it does cause strain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings or difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?\n\nMy sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a social activity because of your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.\n\nBut it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?\n\nAnd at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. Plans, times, who’s bringing what. I don’t think they’re trying to be mean. It’s just that they can’t really count on me to keep it all straight without someone checking behind me.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused strain. Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focusing and restlessness continuing into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they have. Though when you say “adult life,” it makes me think back even farther, honestly. In grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered — that was the phrase, “bright but scattered.” I was always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work.\n\nMy mother kept some of those report cards in a drawer with old birthday cards and recipes clipped from the newspaper — she saved everything. But yes, the same sort of thing didn’t just disappear when I grew up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?\n\nAnd at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. Plans, times, who’s bringing what. I don’t think they’re trying to be mean. It’s just that they can’t really count on me to keep it all straight without someone checking behind me.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused strain. Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focusing and restlessness continuing into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they have. Though when you say “adult life,” it makes me think back even farther, honestly. In grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered — that was the phrase, “bright but scattered.” I was always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work.\n\nMy mother kept some of those report cards in a drawer with old birthday cards and recipes clipped from the newspaper — she saved everything. But yes, the same sort of thing didn’t just disappear when I grew up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your difficulties with focusing and restlessness have significantly impacted your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain a job?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"functional_impairment","name":"Functional Impairment","description":"required","reason":"The doctor is asking whether the focusing/restlessness difficulties have significantly impaired daily functioning or work-related functioning, which directly matches functional impairment."}

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a social activity because of your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.\n\nBut it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?\n\nAnd at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. But yes, the same sort of thing didn’t just disappear when I grew up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your difficulties with focusing and restlessness have significantly impacted your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain a job?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started causing real strain at home and at the community center, because my husband and other volunteers can’t count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has… though I’m not working now, so I can’t really say it’s affecting a job at the moment. But daily things, yes. Keeping track of plans, times, who said what—those things get muddled.

My husband ends up checking behind me, and at the community center they do too. It reminds me of when my sister used to keep this giant wall calendar with everyone’s birthdays in different colored ink—she never missed a thing. I always admired that, but I was never that way.

So yes, it’s causing real strain. Not like everything is falling apart, but people don’t really trust me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks, and that’s hard to sit with.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just feel like I’m always going, even when there’s no real reason to be. Like on a quiet day, I’ll start with the garden club list, then somehow I’m into my knitting bag, and then I’m sorting travel papers before I’ve actually finished any of it. It’s not like I’m doing anything terribly important, but I’m up and moving from one thing to the next all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. Like, I’ll decide to move a plant pot because it looks wrong where it is, and then I’m halfway through wiping the windowsill, and then I remember I left the kettle on—well, not on-on, just boiled already. \n\nSo, impulse… I don’t know. I’d have to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and switching between tasks is causing you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit. At home my husband gets frustrated because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then I’ve gone off into three other things, and he ends up checking behind me.\n\nAnd at the community center too—oh, they’re lovely people, really, we had that bake table last spring and everyone was so patient—but lately I can tell the other volunteers don’t quite trust me to keep the plans straight unless someone else looks them over. That feels embarrassing. It’s not disastrous, but it does cause strain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start out meaning to listen properly, like at a planning meeting, and then a little while later I realize I’ve missed half of what was said. And it’s the same with reading something simple, like the church bulletin—I’ll be looking right at it, but then I don’t really take in what I just read.\n\nThough sometimes I think, well, maybe everyone does that a bit, especially if the room is warm or someone’s talking too long. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. If I have to sit still too long, I get this… stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll tell myself, “Just sit and relax,” but then I’m noticing the magazines on the table are crooked, or I wonder if I left something in the other room. Not always terrible, but it makes me a bit edgy if I can’t get up and do something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it really does go back that far. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I remember my mother keeping all those school notices in a drawer in the kitchen… though half the time I probably forgot to bring them home in the first place. So yes, following along and keeping track of things was hard even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often struggle to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I notice it even now when I help with papers at the community center. I’ll be sure I checked the sign-up sheet, and then later someone says I missed a name, or I put the wrong room time on a little note.\n\nBack in school, I suppose it was that sort of thing too—thinking I’d done it right, but some small thing would be off. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. I just feel like I need to move or tend to something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings or difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated if something’s been misplaced or if I’m trying to explain something and it’s not coming out right… but doesn’t everyone?\n\nMy sister used to say I’d “get worked up,” but she says that about everyone, honestly. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that. Or at least I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a social activity because of your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I haven’t exactly quit, no… not in a formal way. I’m not working now anyway, and at the community center I still go.\n\nBut it has caused strain, I’ll say that. My husband gets frustrated because he’ll ask, “Are you sure that’s the right time?” and I’ll say yes, and then—well, it turns out I mixed up the plan. And at the center, some of the other volunteers kind of check behind me now, which… I understand, but it doesn’t feel very good.\n\nSo I haven’t left because of it, but it’s made things harder. People don’t always feel they can count on me to keep things straight unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit. At home especially, my husband has gotten kind of tired of having to ask, “Did you write that down?” or “Are you sure that’s when we’re supposed to be there?” And then I get defensive, because nobody likes being checked up on, you know?\n\nAnd at the community center, well—there’s this one volunteer who keeps a little clipboard, very organized, always has a pen that works, which is more than I can say for most pens in my purse… but anyway, they’ve started double-checking things after I say them. Plans, times, who’s bringing what. I don’t think they’re trying to be mean. It’s just that they can’t really count on me to keep it all straight without someone checking behind me.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused strain. Not ruined relationships, but it makes me feel… less trusted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focusing and restlessness continuing into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they have. Though when you say “adult life,” it makes me think back even farther, honestly. In grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered — that was the phrase, “bright but scattered.” I was always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work.\n\nMy mother kept some of those report cards in a drawer with old birthday cards and recipes clipped from the newspaper — she saved everything. But yes, the same sort of thing didn’t just disappear when I grew up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with focusing and restlessness have significantly impacted your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain a job?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has… though I’m not working now, so I can’t really say it’s affecting a job at the moment. But daily things, yes. Keeping track of plans, times, who said what—those things get muddled.\n\nMy husband ends up checking behind me, and at the community center they do too. It reminds me of when my sister used to keep this giant wall calendar with everyone’s birthdays in different colored ink—she never missed a thing. I always admired that, but I was never that way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing real strain. Not like everything is falling apart, but people don’t really trust me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks, and that’s hard to sit with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yes, it’s been pretty consistent, I think. I mean, not every single minute of every day, but it didn’t just start recently.

Even way back in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was “bright but scattered.” I remember that phrase because she used to repeat it, usually while looking through my school papers, which were always half-crumpled or missing something. I was forever losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.

So… yes, I guess it’s been there a long time. It just looked a little different depending on what stage of life I was in.

I mean, I do sometimes do things kind of quickly, but “without thinking” sounds… bigger than what I notice. We used to have those meetings in the back room where the chairs squeaked terribly, and I’d notice that instead. But yes… staying with one thing for a long time is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, from what I remember—and from what my mother used to repeat, usually when she was annoyed with me—my teachers in grade school would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nThey’d tell her I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and sort of squirming around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. I can still picture those little wooden desks, actually, with the groove for the pencil… but yes, I suppose I had trouble sitting still even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or irritable when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that way. Even in grade school, my teachers were telling my mother I was bright but scattered—always losing papers, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and wiggling around in my seat before I’d finished my work.\n\nI remember she’d come home from conferences with that look on her face, and I’d know they’d said it again. Funny, the things you remember… the smell of the floor wax in those classrooms. But yes, it’s been most of my life, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Were you often forgetful or did you have trouble following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was, I think. My teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, but scattered—that was the phrase they always seemed to use.\n\nI was always losing papers, and I’d talk out of turn, and I’d be wiggling around in my seat before I’d even finished my work. It’s embarrassing, really, because you feel like you looked right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you exhibit this restlessness and difficulty sitting still in most situations, including when you're at home, at the community center, or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that… not always in some dramatic way, but if I have to sit still too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside. Like I need to get up and straighten a stack of papers, or go check whether I left something in the other room.\n\nAt the community center it happens if there’s a long meeting—oh, those folding chairs are terrible anyway—but I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay put,” and then I’m half-looking for a reason to stand up. At home too, if I’m trying to watch something or sit through a phone call. She once got upset with the grocery clerk over coupons, so I don’t know that she’s the best judge. But… no, I don’t think I have big ups and downs like that.
